1.0

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

INTRODUCTION

David Schaeffer Engineering Limited (DSEL) has been retained to prepare a Functional Servicing
and Stormwater Management Report (FSR) in support of the Innis Lake Secondary Plan area
(G1 Secondary Planning Area) in the Town of Caledon.
The secondary plan is bounded by Mayfield Road to the south, Innis Lake Road to the west,
Centreville Creek Road to the east and Healey Road to the north in the Town of Caledon, Regional
Municipality of Peel. The secondary plan is identified as New Community Area in the 2025 Future
Caledon Official Plan and is shown in Figure 1.
The secondary plan has been divided into two phases of development due to the varying
development timings of the participating landowners. Phase 1 of the secondary plan includes the
lands south of the future east-west collector road, approximately 1.5km north of Mayfield Road.
These lands have been analyzed and discussed in detail in this FSR.
Phase 2 of the secondary plan includes the remaining lands within the Innis Lake Secondary
Planning area which are located north of the Phase 1 lands and extend up to Healey Road. The
Phase 2 lands have been presented conceptually to confirm that the advancement of Phase 1
does not preclude viable servicing and development strategies for the Phase 2 lands. An FSR will
be required for the Phase 2 plans when a development concept is prepared.
This FSR is a high-level document intended to identify the development constraints, SWM targets,
and general site serviceability of the overall secondary plan. This FSR addresses the functional
servicing and stormwater management for the proposed development in accordance with the
Local Subwatershed Study (LSS, RJ Burnside et al., May 2026) and relevant municipal standards.
This FSR will generally confirm that adequate services are available to support the proposed
development in accordance with the standards of the Town of Caledon, Region of Peel, Toronto
Region Conservation Authority (TRCA), and general industry practice.

3.0

PROPOSED LAND USE

The secondary plan lands are currently under agricultural use and include several natural heritage
features associated with tributaries of the West Humber River. The secondary plan area is
approximately 410 Ha, with the Phase 1 lands comprising approximately 154 Ha.
The Concept Plan for the Phase 1 lands includes Neighbourhood Area, Neighbourhood Centre,
Urban Corridor, Natural Heritage System, Stormwater Management Ponds, Neighbourhood
Parks, Elementary and Secondary Schools, and Place of Worship. The development concept,
illustrated in Figure 3, includes the following land uses listed in Table 3-1.

4.0

STORM DRAINAGE

The secondary plan will be developed using a treatment train approach for addressing stormwater
runoff generated by the proposed development, consisting of:
 the application of source control and LID techniques as appropriate, preferentially located
in public areas with appropriate soil conditions to provide stormwater quality control and
address the site water balance.
 conveyance techniques as appropriate; and
 end of pipe facilities such as wet ponds and other stormwater management facilities for
additional quantity, quality, and erosion control.
The pre-development drainage areas and drainage patterns for the secondary plan are discussed
in Section 6.1 of the LSS.
4.1

Floodplain Assessment

An existing conditions floodplain assessment was completed as part of the LSS for the secondary
plan area. Please refer to Section 6.2 and 6.3 of the LSS for detailed discussion of the existing
conditions floodplain assessment.
A post-development floodplain assessment will be completed as part of Part ‘B’ of the LSS to
evaluate the impact of the proposed secondary plan development on the floodplain.
4.2

Proposed Drainage Patterns

Post-development drainage areas have been delineated to reflect the Phase 1 secondary plan
concept area with due consideration for property ownership, community phasing, and
consideration for external drainage patterns entering the subject property. A conventional storm
sewer system and major system conveyance network is designed to direct storm flows safely
through the catchment to designated stormwater management facilities for treatment where
appropriate. The proposed conditions drainage strategy is illustrated in Figure 3.
The number and location of ponds and the associated drainage areas presented in this report are
generally established on technical merits, community phasing, and environmental consideration.
The number of stormwater management ponds have been minimized to reduce maintenance
requirements for the Town and to improve land use efficiency without compromising the benefits

DAVID SCHAEFFER ENGINEERING LTD.

PAGE 8
© DSEL

FUNCTIONAL SERVICING AND SWM REPORT

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

of stormwater management. All SWM requirements would still be met through the proposed SWM
Ponds.
External proposed conditions drainage areas to ponds and to the subject property is addressed
in Section 4.2.1. The grading and drainage boundaries may be further refined at the detailed
design stage.
4.2.1

Boundary Road Drainage and External Drainage

The boundary roads have been reviewed to identify areas that could potentially drain to ponds
within the secondary plan lands for stormwater treatment. Currently, quality control is provided
through grassed swales, with no quantity control measures in place.
Mayfield Road
Mayfield Road is currently a 2-lane rural cross-section and is proposed to be urbanized and
widened as part of the Region of Peel’s Transportation Master Plan (2019). It is understood that
the Region will be widening the road along the frontage of the Phase 1 lands, from the existing
rural 2-lane section to an urbanized 5-lane section, with accompanying storm sewers to collect
and convey road drainage.
The Mayfield Road widening and urbanization is currently in detailed design with construction
anticipated to begin in Summer 2026. The detailed design drawings for Mayfield Road show a
storm sewer network equipped with underground storage and dedicated sewer outlets to provide
quality and quantity control for road runoff. As such, runoff from Mayfield Road has not been
considered in the design of the Innis Lake Secondary plan.
Innis Lake Road
Innis Lake Road is a 2-lane rural cross-section and is proposed to by urbanized and widened from
2 to 4 lanes by 2041 per the Town of Caledon’s Multi-Modal Transportation Master Plan (June
2024). Under existing conditions, road runoff is collected in ditches on either side of the road and
conveyed to TWHRB-5b approximately 100m north of Mayfield Road. Under post-development
conditions, TWHRB-5b is proposed to be eliminated and drainage from Innis Lake Road will be
conveyed via storm sewer to the 1220mm culvert on Mayfield Road. Similar to Mayfield Road, it
is anticipated that the urbanized design of Innis Lake Road will include storm sewers to collect
and convey road drainage to the culvert on Mayfield Road. As part of the secondary plan it is
assumed that the future Innis Lake Road widening design will include provisions for quality and
quantity control.
Centreville Creek Road
Centreville Creek Road is a 2-lane rural cross-section and is proposed to by urbanized and
widened from 2 to 4 lanes by 2041 per the Town of Caledon’s Multi-Modal Transportation Master
Plan (June 2024). Under existing conditions, road runoff is collected in ditches on either side of
the road and conveyed to TWHRA-3b and TWHRA-3b-1 before crossing Mayfield Road via the
1800mm culvert east of Centreville Creek Road. It is anticipated that the urbanized design of
Centreville Creek Road will include storm sewers to collect and convey road drainage to the
existing creek TWHRA-3b and TWHRA-3b-1. As part of the secondary plan, it is assumed that
the future Centreville Creek Road widening design will include provisions for quality and quantity
control.
External Drainage Areas:
The Innis Lake secondary plan area represents the upstream end of the 36.07, 36.08, 36.09,
36.10 and 36.11 catchments. External drainage north of the secondary plan (Healey Road) drains
DAVID SCHAEFFER ENGINEERING LTD.

© DSEL

FUNCTIONAL SERVICING AND SWM REPORT

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

to the Phase 2 lands via culverts on Healey Road and Centreville Creek Road. This external
drainage is generally conveyed directly to the natural valley system within the Phase 2 lands and
has been included in the hydrology modeling. External drainage west of the secondary plan is
captured in existing culverts on Innis Lake Road at the northern limit of the Phase 1 lands and
conveyed to TWHRB-8.
External drainage east of the secondary plan will be conveyed to the natural creek systems
TWHRA-3b and TWHRA-3b-1 via culverts on Centreville Creek Road and Mayfield Road.
Phase 1 will be developed prior to Phase 2. Cut-off swales will be installed at the development
limit between Phase 1 and Phase 2 to direct drainage to TWHRB-7b, TWHRA-7, and TWHRA6a. Once Phase 2 proceeds with development, SWM ponds will be required within the Phase 2
lands to provide both quantity and quality control.
4.3

Conveyance of Minor System Flows

The secondary plan will generally be serviced by a conventional storm sewer system designed in
accordance with Town of Caledon standards:
 Storm sewers will be sized using a 10-year return frequency without surcharging where
foundation drains will be connected to the storm sewer.
 Storm sewers will be sized using a 5-year return frequency where foundation drains will
not be connected to the minor system (i.e. sump pumps are utilized).
Sump pumps will be required in areas of shallow storm sewers or where 0.3 m of freeboard is not
achieved from the 100 year / Regional hydraulic grade line to the underside of footing elevation.
The sump pump outlet locations will be established through detailed design and will require back
flow preventers or gooseneck connections to discharge to the storm sewer.
Given grading constraints associated with boundary roads and natural heritage systems,
combined with the requirement for Regional control ponds and the resulting pond water levels
and hydraulic grade line, sump pumps are anticipated within the majority of the Phase 1 lands.
As such, in accordance with Section 1.4.2.2.2 of the Town standards, the storm sewers are to be
sized using a 5-year return frequency.
Storm flows from Phase 1 lands will be directed to one of the seven proposed stormwater
management ponds (SWMP) or one of two proposed On-site Control Areas (OSCAs) in the study
area, where the runoff will be treated for water quality, erosion, and quantity control. OSCAs are
discussed further in Section 5.0
The conceptual storm servicing plan is provided in Drawing 2 and the overall drainage plan
identifying catchment areas to the respective ponds is provided in Figure 5. Preliminary plans
and profiles are shown on Drawing 4. Preliminary storm design sheets are included in Appendix
B.
4.4

Conveyance of Major Storm Flows

A continuous overland flow route will be provided through Phase 1 in order to safely convey major
system flows in excess of the minor system and up to the 100-year event. Overland flow routes
will be directed to one of seven stormwater management ponds or two OSCAs located within the
study area. The major system flow will not exceed the width of the road allowance, and in no
case will the depth of flow exceed 0.30 meters above the gutter of the road during a 100-year
event, in accordance with the Town of Caledon criteria.
DAVID SCHAEFFER ENGINEERING LTD.

PAGE 10
© DSEL

FUNCTIONAL SERVICING AND SWM REPORT
MAY 2026

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

Should the major system flow exceed the conveyance capacity of any given road, the storm sewer
will be sized to accommodate the flows in excess of the road capacity.
The major system flows will be attenuated in the stormwater management ponds or OSCAs to
achieve the allowable release rates as defined in Section 5.3.4.
4.4.1

Uncontrolled Areas

Driven primarily by grading constraints within Phase 1, there are circumstances that result in
major system flows that are unable to be attenuated in the stormwater management ponds. These
scenarios include:
 Rear yards backing onto the Natural Heritage System
o

Rear yards are often >1.5m lower than the adjacent road grade and as such it is
not practical to capture rear yard drainage into the storm sewer

o

This drainage is considered to be clean drainage and does not require quality
control

 Areas directed to On-site Control Areas (OSCAs) will be provided with quality controls and
quantity controls up to the 100yr storm (not including Regional)
o

There are two areas on the southeast corner of the plan that are bounded by NHS
and / or external roads on all sides

o

These areas are 5 Ha in size or less and as such do not merit a SWM pond for
quality and quantity control

o

On-site control within these development blocks will be provided for quality control
and quantity control up to and including the 100yr storm

o

The ultimate location and details of these on-site control areas will be determined
through site-specific draft plan applications

An assessment has been completed to demonstrate these uncontrolled areas can discharge to
the West Humber River without increasing the total peak flows from the secondary plan. As such,
no overcontrol is required in the ponds. “Part B” of the LSS will include discussion of the postdevelopment hydrology and analysis of the downstream Flood Vulnerable Areas (FVA).
5.0

STORMWATER MANAGEMENT

Stormwater management for Phase 1 of the secondary plan will be accommodated in seven
stormwater management facilities (SWMs) and two on-site control areas (OSCAs). All SWM
ponds will be conventional wet ponds. Details for the OGS units provided as part of the OSCAs
will be provided at detailed design and will be designed to provide 80% TSS removal (i.e. Jellyfish
Unit TM, or equivalent) for the upstream catchment. Details of the stormwater management for
the Phase 2 lands will be part of a future study for the proposed development of Phase 2.
SWM Pond 1 is located at the northwest corner in Phase 1 of the secondary plan area.
The pond is bounded by the proposed east-west collector road to the north, a proposed
local road to the west, and the proposed realigned channel to the east and south. The
pond is situated at an existing low point near the confluence of TWHRA-7a and TWHRB8. The pond will provide quality and quantity control for approximately 4.8 Ha of lands
within Phase 1 and in the future development within Phase 2. The pond outfall will be
located on the eastern portion of the pond and will discharge to the proposed realigned
channel.
DAVID SCHAEFFER ENGINEERING LTD.

PAGE 11
© DSEL

FUNCTIONAL SERVICING AND SWM REPORT
MAY 2026

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

SWM Pond 2 is bounded by the existing NHS to the south and east, a proposed collector
road to the west, and proposed residential lots to the north. The pond will provide quality
and quantity control for approximately 22.5 Ha, and discharge to the existing channel
TWHRB-7 to the east.
SWM Pond 3 is located in the southwest portion of the secondary plan, bounded by
proposed local roads to the north and west, existing NHS to the east, and the existing
Place of Worship to the south. The pond will provide quality and quantity control for
approximately 9.3 Ha, discharging to the existing channel TWHRB-6 to the east.
SWM Pond 4 is bounded by the existing NHS to the west, a proposed collector road to
the north and east, and proposed residential lots to the south. The pond will provide quality
and quantity control for approximately 12.3 Ha, discharging to the existing channel
TWHRB-7 to the west.
SWM Pond 5 is located at the southern portion of the secondary plan, bounded by
Mayfield Road to the south, a proposed collector road to the east, a local road to the north,
and future residential lots to the west. The pond will provide quality and quantity control
for approximately 8.5 Ha, and discharge to the existing channel TWHRB-5 to the east.
SWM Pond 6 is located at the northeast portion in Phase 1 of the secondary plan,
bounded by a proposed park to the north, existing NHS to the west, a proposed collector
road to the south, and a proposed local road to the east. The pond will provide quality and
quantity control for approximately 34.6 Ha, discharging to the existing channel TWHRA-6
to the west.
SWM Pond 7 is located at the southeast portion of the secondary plan, bounded by
existing NHS to the west and south, a proposed local road to the north, and future
residential lots to the south and east. The pond will provide quality and quantity control for
approximately 6.3 Ha, and discharge to the existing channel TWHRA-3c to the west.
OSCA 1 will be located in the southern portion of the secondary plan. The drainage area
to OSCA 1 is approximately 5.2 Ha and is bounded by the existing NHS corridors
associated with TWHRA-6, TWHRA-3b, and TWHRA-3b1. This area is unable to drain to
one of the seven ponds described above and will require its own on-site control area for
quality and quantity control. Given that the drainage area is at the minimum allowable
drainage area for a conventional wet pond (i.e. 5 Ha), details for the OSCA will be
investigated through a site-specific FSR as part of the draft plan application for these
lands.
OSCA 2 will be located in the southeastern portion of the secondary plan. The drainage
area to OSCA 2 is approximately 2.9 Ha and is bounded by Centreville Creek Road,
Mayfield Road, and the existing NHS corridors associated with TWHRA-3b and TWHRA3b1. This area is unable to drain to one of the seven ponds described above and will
require its own on-site control area for quality and quantity control. Given that the drainage
area is below the minimum allowable drainage area for a conventional wet pond (i.e. 5
Ha), details for the OSCA will be investigated through a site-specific FSR as part of the
draft plan application for these lands.
Clay liner recommendations for all SWM ponds will be determined through a site-specific
geotechnical investigation. The following sections outline the preliminary details for Ponds 1
through 7, and the two OSCA areas within the Phase 1 lands.

5.1

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

Design Criteria

Stormwater management within the secondary plan is proposed in accordance with the criteria
set out in Section 6.4 of the LSS summarized below:
Table 5-1: Summary of Stormwater Management Criteria
Water Quality Control
 Provide Enhanced (Level 1) water quality treatment, sized in
accordance with the SWMP Manual.
 90th percentile storm event addressed through the CLI-ECA
hierarchy requirements.
Erosion Control

 Provide adequate drawdown time / erosion control to protect
the form and function of watercourses downstream of the
SWM facilities as per TRCA requirements of a 48-hour
drawdown time for the 25 mm storm runoff volume

Quantity Control

 Volume required to meet unitary rates from the TRCA’s
Humber River Hydrology reports based on postdevelopment drainage area to SWM facility.
 Quantity control required for the 2- to 100-year and Regional
events in accordance with the unit flow equations for
subcatchments 36.07 through 36.11 of Humber, as provided
by TRCA Quantity Controls are required for 2- to 100-year and
Regional storm events.

All facilities will be designed accordingly to meet the criteria in Section 4.2.1 of the TRCA’s
Approaches to Manage Regulatory Event Flow Increases resulting from Urban Development
(TRCA, 2016), where applicable.
All facilities will be designed to generally meet the criteria in Appendix E of the TRCA’s Stormwater
Management Criteria (TRCA, 2012), where applicable.
The stormwater management for the subject area is to be designed with a treatment train
approach, with the catchment upstream of the ponds deploying LIDs. Furthermore, the study
area is intended to comply with the Consolidated Linear Infrastructure Environmental Compliance
Approval (CLI-ECA) criteria outlined in Table 5-2. The CLI-ECA criteria is intended to allow
development applications to proceed with ECAs under the direction of local municipalities through
a delegated authority from the Province of Ontario.
5.1.1

CLI-ECA Design Criteria

The MECP published design criteria for sanitary sewers, storm sewers, and forcemains
authorized under Environmental Compliance Approval (ECA) in May 2023. The Consolidated
Linear Infrastructure – Environmental Compliance Approval (CLI-ECA) process is delegated
authority from the Province of Ontario (MECP) to municipalities through an agreement to modify
or expand their infrastructure networks, subject to provisions outlined in the agreement with the
MECP. As part of the CLI-ECA agreement process, the MECP has issued design performance
criteria within the agreement with municipalities pertaining to stormwater management objectives,

as shown in Table 5.2. However, the Performance Criteria has not been translated to Town of
Caledon design standards and details that can be used by practitioners.

heritage, Ecologically significant Groundwater Recharge Areas (EGRA), inflow and infiltration
strategies. The assessment should include sufficient detail to be used at a local site level and
consistent with the various level of studies; OR
IF Assessment Studies in i) NOT completed:
ii) Control [3] the recharge [4] to meet Pre-development [5] conditions on property; OR
iii) Control [3] the runoff from the 90th percentile storm event.
Lake Simcoe Watershed Municipalities:
iv) Control [3] as per the evaluation of anticipated changes in water balance between Predevelopment and post-development assessed through a Stormwater management plan in
support of an application for Major Development [6]. The assessment should include sufficient
detail to be used at a local site level. If it is demonstrated, using the approved water balance
estimation methods [7], that the site’s post to Pre-development water balance cannot be met,
and Maximum Extent Possible [8] has been attained, the proponent may use Lake Simcoe and
Region Conservation Authority’s (LSRCA) Recharge Compensation Program [9].
FOR RETROFIT SCENARIOS [10]
Assessment Studies:
i) Control as per criteria identified in the water balance assessment completed in one or more of
the following studies: a watershed/subwatershed plan, Source Protection Plan (Assessment
Report component), Master Stormwater Management Plan, Master Environmental Servicing
Plan, Class EA, or local site study including natural heritage, EGRA, inflow and infiltration
strategies, if undertaken. The assessment should include sufficient detail to be used at a local
site level and consistent with the various level of studies; OR
ii) If constraints [11] identified in i), then control [3] as per Maximum Extent Possible [8] based on
environmental site feasibility studies or address local needs[14].
IF Assessment Studies in i) NOT completed:
iii) Control [3] the recharge [4] to meet Pre-development [5] conditions on property; OR
Control [3] the runoff from the 90th percentile storm event.

Development includes new development, redevelopment, infill development, or conversion of a rural cross-section into an urban cross-section.
Stormwater volumes generated from the geographically specific 90th percentile rainfall event on an annual average basis from all surfaces on the entire site are targeted
for control. Control is in the following hierarchical order, with each step exhausted before proceeding to the next: 1) retention (infiltration, reuse, or evapotranspiration), 2)
LID filtration, and 3) conventional Stormwater management. Step 3, conventional Stormwater management, should proceed only once Maximum Extent Possible [8] has
been attained for Steps 1 and 2 for retention and filtration.
Recharge is the infiltration and movement of surface water into the soil, past the vegetation root zone, to the zone of saturation, or water table.
Pre-development is defined as the more stringent of the two following scenarios: 1) a site’s existing condition, or 2) as defined by the local municipality.
Major Development has the same meaning as in the Lake Simcoe Protection Plan, 2009.
Currently, the approved tool by LSRCA for calculating the water balance is the Thornthwaite-Mather Method. Other tools agreed upon by relevant approval agencies (e.g.,

requirements of the Town of Caledon Design Standards, TRCA Hydrology Updates, and the
SWMP Manual, and include the following features:
Sediment Forebay

to improve sediment removal prior to entering the pond

Permanent Pool

to provide water quality and trap pollutants

Extended Detention Storage

to provide erosion control

Quantity Control Storage

to attenuate post development flows to the allowable
release rates as per the TRCA Hydrology Updates

5.3

POND COMPONENTS

5.3.1

Sediment Forebay

DAVID SCHAEFFER ENGINEERING LTD.

PAGE 20
© DSEL

FUNCTIONAL SERVICING AND SWM REPORT
MAY 2026

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

A sediment forebay is provided in each stormwater management pond. The SWM Pond designs
include a sediment forebay in order to improve the pollutant removal by trapping larger particles
near the inlet of the pond. The forebays have been designed with a length to width ratio of
approximately 3:1 and do not exceed one third of the permanent pool surface area for wet ponds,
as required in the MOECC SWMP Design Manual. Furthermore, the forebay has a minimum
depth of 1.5m to minimize the potential for re-suspension.
5.3.2

Permanent Pool

The permanent pools will be approximately 3m deep. The permanent pools have been sized to
provide Enhanced Level protection in accordance with the MOECC SWMP Design Manual.
Slopes of 7:1 (H:V) or flatter will be provided for 3m (horizontally) on either side of the permanent
pool wetted perimeter.
The storage characteristics are summarized in Table 5-4.

The extended detention component has been provided with side slopes of 7:1 with minor localized

The extended detention volumes within the ponds will outlet through a reverse graded pipe. An

per the SWMP Manual is 50mm. If this is not possible, an alternative option such as using a
custom inlet control device (e.g. Hydrovex) may be reviewed at the detailed design stage.

Emergency Overflows

In the event of a blockage or a storm greater than the Regional Event, an emergency overflow
weir has been provided in all SWM ponds. The emergency overflow weirs will be sized through
detailed design to convey the greater of the unattenuated 100 year or Regional inflow to the pond.
5.3.6

Access Roads

DAVID SCHAEFFER ENGINEERING LTD.

PAGE 23
© DSEL

FUNCTIONAL SERVICING AND SWM REPORT
MAY 2026

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

5.0 m wide access roads will be provided on at least two sides of the pond blocks to facilitate
routine inspection and maintenance activities within the pond. The access roads will be graded
with a maximum slope of 2% cross slope, and 10:1 longitudinal slope for the access road into the
pond forebay and main cell.
The pond access roads will connect to municipal right-of-ways, and will allow for access to the
outlet structure, inlet structures, forebay and main cell. The maintenance access roads will be
configured such that two points of entry are provided where possible. The access roads shall be
situated in a manner that allows trucks to drive around the pond without having to turn around.
5.3.7

Sediment Drying Area

The Town has varied criteria for sediment drying areas depending on whether sediment removal
operations will occur in dry conditions or wet conditions. Sediment drying areas will be provided
above the 2-year water level to allow for sediment removal. The sediment drying areas will be
sized to provide a sediment drying volume for a minimum of 10 years of sediment accumulation.
5.3.8 Pond Outfall
All ponds will discharge to the West Humber River. All outfalls to the West Humber River will be
designed to generally meet the criteria in Appendix E2 of the TRCA’s Stormwater Management
Criteria (TRCA, 2012), where applicable.
The outfalls are proposed to discharge to the valley floor, given the bank steepness. The outfalls
will provide energy dissipation at the headwall to mitigate localized erosive forces in the floodplain
and valley floor. Outfalls and plunge pools are generally oriented perpendicular to the adjacent
stream to minimize risk of channel bank scour, and opportunities to have all outfalls oriented in
this manner to the extent feasible will be explored at detailed design. The details of the energy
dissipator or plunge pool will be provided at detailed design. Construction of the outfalls should
minimize disturbance to the Natural Heritage System.
The outfalls will generally be located as close to the toe of valley slope as possible to be as far
from the low-flow channel as possible. Furthermore, the outfalls will be oriented at an obtuse
angle in the downstream direction to avoid perpendicular flows from entering the low flow channel,
where possible.
5.4

POND MAINTENANCE

The following section provides general guidance on pond maintenance for the secondary plan.
Further refinement and details can be provided at detailed design.
5.4.1

Inspections

As recommended in the SWMP Manual, inspections should be made after every significant storm
(e.g., >10 mm) during the first two years of operation to ensure that the facility is functioning
properly. It is anticipated that four inspections will be required per year. After the initial period,
and proper operation has been confirmed, an inspection schedule can be established based on
the observed operation of the ponds. Although four inspections per year are recommended, the
ponds should be inspected annually as a minimum requirement.
5.4.2

Regular Operation and Maintenance Activities

DAVID SCHAEFFER ENGINEERING LTD.

PAGE 24
© DSEL

FUNCTIONAL SERVICING AND SWM REPORT
MAY 2026

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

Grass Cutting
Grass cutting is not recommended for the ponds. Allowing grass to grow enhances the water
quality and provides other benefits. It is understood though, that grass cutting enhances the
aesthetics of the facility for nearby residents and therefore, should be done as infrequently as
possible.
Grass should not be cut to the edge of the permanent pool and should be done parallel to the
shoreline. Grass clippings should be ejected away from the pond.
Weed Control
If weed control is required to remove a specific species, the weeds should be removed by hand.
Plantings
Different parts of the SWM ponds will require different vegetative treatments for upland, flood,
shoreline and aquatic conditions. Planting methods and any replanting should be carried out in
accordance with an approved Landscape Design and the recommendations of the SWMP
Manual, or as modified by the operating authority.
Trash Removal
Trash and debris should be removed by hand and performed as required based on inspections.
Sediment Removal
In accordance with the SWMP Manual, it is recommended that the frequency of sediment removal
be determined based on a 5% reduction in the total suspended solids (TSS) removal efficiency.
The frequency of pond maintenance will be determined at the detailed design stage. It should be
noted that routine cleaning of the sediment forebay should allow for less frequent cleaning of the
main cell than indicated in the SWMP Manual. However, the extension of service life prior to
cleaning cannot be quantified.
Safety
The ponds should be provided with appropriate signage which warns the public of the presence
of deep water and slopes. Landscape drawings are to be prepared with strategic plantings around
the perimeter of the ponds to discourage direct access to the facility. All inlets, outlets, structures,
and headwalls will be provided with the appropriate grates, covers, and safety features to prevent
public entry or tampering.
6.0

WATER BALANCE MITIGATION MEASURES (LID DESIGN)

Where feasible, LID measures for SWM will be incorporated into the development design to
minimize development impacts on the natural water balance and control runoff. The basic
premise for LID is to manage stormwater to minimize the runoff of rainfall and increase the
potential for infiltration through the use of various design techniques. As outlined in the SWMP
Design Manual (2003), the most recent Low Impact Development Stormwater Management
Planning and Design Guide published by the CVC and TRCA and Sustainable Technologies
Evaluation Program LID Stormwater Management webpage, there are a suite of LID techniques
that can be considered to match pre-development infiltration volumes.
DAVID SCHAEFFER ENGINEERING LTD.

PAGE 25
© DSEL

FUNCTIONAL SERVICING AND SWM REPORT
MAY 2026

6.1

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

Water Quantity

There are number of techniques that maximize water availability in pervious areas that are not
credited through the CLI-ECA program. This includes measures located on private property such
as direction of roof runoff towards pervious areas and increased topsoil depth within private lots.
These types of surface LID techniques promote natural infiltration simply by providing additional
water volumes in the pervious areas (i.e., these areas would receive precipitation as well as extra
water from roof runoff). This may be particularly effective in the summer months, when natural
infiltration would not generally occur because the additional water overcomes the natural soil
moisture deficit. While these techniques are not credited through the CLI-ECA program, they do
provide a water quantity benefit by reducing runoff.
In addition to the techniques mentioned above, there are a number of LID measures that can be
implemented as part of the CLI-ECA program. These include measures within areas owned or
maintained by the municipality. To determine the optimal LID strategy, the proposed design must
first consider site constraints. As part of the CLI-ECA program, the MECP provides a checklist of
stormwater management site constraints that should be considered when evaluating if LID
measures are suitable to meet the water balance and/ or quality control targets. Table 8.9.1 from
the Environmental Compliance Approvals for a Stormwater Management System (MECP, 2022)
has been provided below for reference of site constraints.

The use of large, engineered facilities and constructed subsurface infiltration measures such as
infiltration trenches will be limited in where they can be applied due to high groundwater and low
infiltration rates. Figure 15 illustrates the clearance between groundwater and proposed grades
within the site. As noted in Table 6-1 above, and the guiding documents noted in Table 8.9.1 from
the MECP document, 1m clearance is required between the high groundwater elevation and the
bottom of the proposed LID measures to ensure that they are able to function properly. LID
measures have therefore been proposed in areas where 1m separation can be provided between
groundwater and the bottom of the LID measure. Groundwater elevations should be confirmed
through additional testing at detailed design.
6.2

LID DESIGN CONSIDERATIONS

6.2.1

Soil Characteristics and Infiltration Rates

The CVC LID Design Guide recommends that testing be completed to determine the infiltration
rate of native soils to help determine appropriate LID measure locations. Based on hydraulic
conductivity provided in Section 4 of the LSS, the infiltration rate within the Phase 1 lands is
estimated to be 12mm/hr. The CVC LID Design Guide recommends a safety correction factor be
applied to the measured infiltration rates in order to calculate a design infiltration rate. The safety
correction factor is used to compensate for potential reductions in soil permeability to compaction
or smearing during construction, gradual accumulation of fine sediments over the life span of the
trench, and uncertainty in measured values when less permeable soil horizons exist below the
proposed bottom elevation of the trench. Safety factors generally range from 2.5 to 6.5, depending
on the infiltration rates measured. As such, LID measures are assumed to have an infiltration rate
of 12 mm/hr, with a factor of safety of 2.5 applied, resulting in a design infiltration rate of 4.8
mm/hr. It is recommended that, at detailed design, infiltration tests are completed per
recommendations by the LID Design Guide to confirm the Safety Correction Factor that should
be applied.
6.2.2

LID Depth and Drawdown Time

The preliminary LID measure sizing and drawdown times were calculated using the LID Design
Guide within the Phase 1 lands and detailed in Appendix D. The design guidelines have been
used to calculate the allowable reservoir depths of the trenches, as well as the required trench
footprints. The LID sizing is based on a drawdown of designed storage volume within 48 hours
with a void ratio of 0.4 and the adjusted infiltration rate as discussed above. The parameters of
this preliminary infiltration trench sizing should be confirmed and refined through detailed design.

𝑑

The maximum trench depth, required trench footprint, and time to drain calculations are found in
Appendix D. The actual size of trenches, configuration, applicability, design storm, infiltration
rates, depth to water table and frost cover should be refined through detailed design.
6.2.3

Drainage Area

The CVC LID Design Guide recommends an impervious drainage area to treatment facility area
ratio of between 5:1 and 20:1 to avoid too much sediment load being directed to LIDs. Catchment
areas to the LIDs can be found in Figure 15. Pre-treatment, such as catchbasin shields, are
proposed in areas where the impervious drainage area to treatment facility area ratio is greater
than 20:1.
6.2.4

Accessibility for Maintenance

LID location selection is important, and LID design should have consideration for future access
and maintenance of the LID measures.
6.3

TYPES OF LID MEASURES

The current Town of Caledon design guidelines do not include recommended LID measures or
details that would be acceptable in public spaces. As such, the types of LID measures identified
in this section of the report are based on review of LID measures within the Phase 1 lands from
a first-principles approach starting with site constraints. The design of the LID measures is
constrained by clearance to groundwater, design infiltration rates, proposed land use, and
maximum storage depths to achieve 48-hour drawdown time. Typically, two different types of LID
measures are implemented within developments:
 Infiltration Galleries – Within Parks
 Infiltration Trenches – Within select Rights-of-Way
6.3.1

Infiltration Galleries in Parks

As shown in Figure 15, there are three parks proposed within Phase 1 of the secondary plan.
Based on the latest groundwater contour information, there is not adequate clearance from
potential LID measures to groundwater. Detailed groundwater information within the park blocks
should be investigated through draft plan and detailed designs to confirm if LIDs can be
implemented within the park blocks.
Construction Requirements
The proposed infiltration trenches should be constructed during the park servicing. The LID
measures should be kept offline until these areas have been stabilized.
Maintenance and Monitoring Requirements
DAVID SCHAEFFER ENGINEERING LTD.

PAGE 29
© DSEL

FUNCTIONAL SERVICING AND SWM REPORT
MAY 2026

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

Inspection and clean-out ports should be included to ensure minimal maintenance. If required,
replacement of components will be carried out through a trench box or open cut, depending on
park programming. The LID measures are located near the adjacent roadways to provide
unobstructed access to the LIDs from the road. A monitoring program should be developed
through detailed design in consultation with the Town of Caledon.
6.3.2

Infiltration Trenches in the ROW

As shown in Figure 15, infiltration trenches are proposed within rights-of-way throughout the
Phase 1 lands. LIDs in the ROW require a minimum of 3.5m clearance between groundwater and
surface grades to provide 1m clearance from groundwater to the bottom of the gallery. The
proposed infiltration trenches will be connected to manholes within the road. Pre-treatment will be
provided in the catchbasins using CB shields, before runoff is directed to the storm sewer/trench.
The trench will be installed below the storm sewer so that at the manhole, flows can be directed
towards the trench and spill to the storm sewer when the capacity is exceeded. This also allows
upstream catchments to be directed to the downstream infiltration trenches. Tables 6-2 provides
design data and infiltration volumes.

Construction Requirements
The proposed infiltration trenches should be constructed during the site servicing and road

Maintenance and Monitoring Requirements
Manholes will function as inspection and clean-out ports, and removable maintenance caps will

components will be carried out through a trench box to limit disturbance within the right-of-way. A

of Caledon.

The storage provided by all possible locations of LID measures represents 7.7% of the 90 th
percentile storm event volume, or the equivalent of approximately the 2mm event. To achieve
more than 2% of the 90th Percentile storm event target, the LIDs would need to infiltrate more
than existing conditions infiltration. The Town of Caledon would need to consider potential
environmental impacts of infiltrating more water than pre-development conditions, as well as the
cost implications of additional LIDs. Alternative quality-based LID strategies could be explored
such as waterproofed trenches to restrict groundwater interaction, but the Town of Caledon must
consider the benefits of the additional LID measures compared to the cost of installation and
maintenance for additional infrastructure. In any case, it is not feasible to provide the entire 90 th
percentile storm event volume through infiltration LID measures.

10.0

ROADS

Access to the secondary plan is available from the north via Healey Road, from the south via
Mayfield Road, and the west and east via Innis Lake Road and Centreville Creek Road
respectively.
The community will include a series of 18 m and 26m wide municipal roads and 8m wide laneways
in accordance with Town of Caledon standards. Sidewalks will be constructed in accordance with
the standard municipal cross sections. Sidewalk locations may be further refined through detailed
design. Please refer to Figure 14A to Figure 14F for typical rights-of-way sections within Phase
1 of the secondary plan.
11.0

GRADING

A preliminary grading plan has been prepared for the Phase 1 lands based on the environmental
and engineering constraints identified. The proposed road grades range from 0.75% to 5%. The
conceptual grading is illustrated in Drawing 1.
Interim grading measures may be required adjacent to the Phase 2 lands north of the east-west
collector road, until the Phase 2 lands proceed for development.
11.1

Grading in Natural Heritage System

Grading in the NHS buffers is minimized but required at the following locations:
 Two (2) stormwater management ponds
 Four (4) areas along the perimeter of the site where the road grade is higher than the
existing NHS grade
The anticipated grading within the NHS buffers is illustrated in Drawing 1.
The grading in the NHS has been minimized through use of walk-out units (approximately 2.5 m
vertically transitions through the lots), and berming within rear-yard where space is available.
Retaining walls have not been used in addition to walk-out unit product type. The remaining
vertical difference is addressed through transition sloping in the buffer to a maximum of 3:1
sloping. Transition sloping is limited to the hazard buffers and is not proposed in buffers
themselves.

13.0

EROSION AND SEDIMENT CONTROL

An erosion and sediment control strategy should be implemented prior to construction of site
services. The following measures are recommended:
Erosion
 regular inspection and monitoring of the erosion and sediment control devices.
 ensure stabilized soil and slope stability to prevent runoff and stability hazards.
 cover non-active work faces of stockpiles, excavation areas, all other exposed surfaces of
concern with tarps or other appropriate materials as practicable.
 Monitoring the weather forecast daily and plan/schedule work activities with specific
consideration of impending weather conditions to prevent events leading towards further
erosion.
 Minimize staging and temporary stockpile of soil and other materials to the extent possible.
Sediment
 silt control fencing and double row silt control fencing with straw bales where required.
 stone mud mat at the construction entrance.
 interceptor swale with rock check dam where appropriate.
 use of the permanent ponds as a temporary silt basin during site construction activities.
 removal and disposal of the erosion and sediment control devices after the site has been
stabilized.
DAVID SCHAEFFER ENGINEERING LTD.

INNIS LAKE SECONDARY PLAN

CONCLUSIONS

This Functional Servicing and Stormwater Management Report provides an overview of the
servicing plan for the secondary plan. This report generally demonstrates the availability of water,
sanitary, and storm services for the proposed development in accordance with Town of Caledon,
Region of Peel, and TRCA criteria and in consideration of applicable guideline documents.
We trust you will find the contents of this report satisfactory.
Prepared by,
David Schaeffer Engineering Ltd.

Hydraulic Capacity and Modeling Analysis
Caledon Block G1 Development

1 Introduction
GeoAdvice Engineering Inc. (GeoAdvice) was retained by David Schaeffer Engineering Ltd. (DSEL)
to complete the hydraulic modeling and pipe network sizing of the Caledon Block G1
(Development) located in the Town of Caledon, Region of Peel (Region).
The Caledon G1 development is located north of Mayfield Road and between Innis Lake Road
and Centreville Creek Road in Caledon, ON. The site is shown in Figure 1.1 on the following page,
illustrating the site layout, proposed water distribution system and connection points.
The development will occur in three phases:
• Interim 2026: Mattamy Development only (one (1) connection to the system on Innis Lake
Road). Total residential population of 4,672.
• Full 2026: Expands upon Interim 2026 by incorporating the rest of the Block G1
Development. The development population of the northern half of Block G1 is assumed
to be the same as the southern half. Total residential population of 30,348.
• Future 2031: Includes the development demands of the Block G2 development (assumed
to have the same development population as Block G1. Point load allocation along
Centreville Creek Road).

Site Layout

Figure 1.1

Hydraulic Capacity and Modeling Analysis
Caledon Block G1 Development

The development will be connected to Pressure Zone 6, as summarized below:
Scenario 1 – Interim 2026 (Mattamy Development Only):
• One (1) connection to the proposed 400 mm main on Innis Lake Road
Scenario 2 – Full 2026 (Complete Block G1 Development):
Innis Lake Road – Two (2) Connections:
• One (1) connection to the 400 mm main from the Mattamy connection
• One (1) connection to the existing 200 mm main on the south side of Innis Lake Road
Mayfield Road – Four (4) Connections:
• All four (4) connections will connect to the existing 750 mm main within Pressure Zone 6
Centreville Creek Road – Three (3) Connections:
• Three (3) connections to the existing 200 mm main
Scenario 3 – Future 2031 (Complete Block G1 and G2 Developments):
The connections of the Future 2031 scenario will be the same as the Full 2026 scenario with the
addition of a proposed trunk main connecting Innis Lake Road and Centreville Creek Road.
This memo describes the assumptions and results of the hydraulic modeling and capacity analysis
using the Region of Peel InfoWater Pro water model dated on January 24, 2023 (Autodesk
Software). InfoWater Pro is a GIS water distribution system modeling and management software
application. The development water network was incorporated into the Region model in order
to complete the hydraulic and sizing analysis.
The results presented in this memo are based on the analysis of steady state simulations. The
predicted available fire flows, as calculated by the hydraulic model, represent the flow available
in the water main while maintaining a residual pressure of 22 psi at the hydrant. No extended
period simulations were completed in this analysis to assess the water quality or to assess the
hydraulic capacity of storage and pumping facilities in Pressure Zone 6.

2 Demands
2.1 Consumer Demands
To estimate the total development population requiring water service, population data were first
calculated in accordance with the development’s site plan. Population estimates were then
multiplied by the rates provided in the Region of Peel’s Development Charges Background Study
(2020).
The assumptions used in estimating water demand for the development are summarized in Table
2.1.

The demands for the northern portion of Block G1 were assumed to be the same as those

calculations are provided in Appendix A.

2.2 Fire Flows
Fire flow demands were provided by DSEL on February 24, 2026 and are as follows:
• Neighbourhood Area: 9,000 L/min (150 L/s)
• Urban Corridor: 12,000 L/min (200 L/s)
• Neighbourhood Centre: 12,000 L/min (200 L/s)
Fire flow simulations were completed at each model node. The locations of nodes do not
necessarily represent hydrant locations. The spatial allocation of the required fire flows is shown
in Appendix B.

3 Modeling Considerations
3.1 Water Main Configuration
The water main network was laid out based on the development layout prepared by David
Schaeffer Engineering (1297_wmServ_2026-02-26.dwg) and provided to GeoAdvice on February
26, 2026.

3.2 Pressure Requirements
As outlined in the Region of Peel Design Guidelines, the generally accepted best practice is to
design new water distribution systems to operate between 40 psi and 100 psi. Pressure
requirements are outlined in Table 3.1.
Table 3.1: Region of Peel Pressure Requirements

3.3 Elevations
Elevations of the modeled junctions were assumed to be 230 m as agreed upon by DSEL on March
4, 2026.

3.4 Pipe Characteristics
Pipe diameters and Hazen-Williams C-factors were assigned in the model according to the Region
of Peel Design Guidelines and the Ministry of Environment. Pipe characteristics used for the
development are outlined in Table 3.2 below.

Hydraulic Capacity and Modeling Analysis
Caledon Block G1 Development

4 Modeling Results
The proposed water mains within the development were sized to the minimum diameter which
would satisfy the greater of maximum day plus fire and peak hour demand. Modeling was carried
out for peak hour and maximum day plus fire flow using InfoWater Pro.

4.1 Pressure Analysis
The modeling results indicate that the development can be adequately serviced by the proposed
water main layout with the recommended sizes. Modeled service pressures for the development
are summarized in Table 4.1 and Table 4.2 below.
Table 4.1: Summary of Pressures (ADD)
Scenario
Interim 2026
Full 2026

The average pressure difference between the Interim 2026 and Full 2026 scenarios is caused by
the activation of Pump #1 at the Wildfield Pump Station in the Full 2026 scenario.
As outlined in the Region of Peel Design Guidelines, the generally accepted best practice is to
design new water distribution systems to operate between 40 psi and 100 psi. As summarized in
the table above, the model predicts that ADD pressures do not exceed 100 psi. As such, local
PRVs may not be required within the development. Refer to Appendix C for detailed ADD
pressure map results.

results.
